Appendix 6.B
Basic Constructions in
Bel

This appendix verifies that the basic constructions in
Bel
from

Section

2
are well-defined.

1
Tensor Product

The tensor product given in

Equations

17
,
18
and
19
is only well-defined if
the domain of the backward map is well-defined, and if the error lens
properties hold. We check these properties below, and restate the definition of
the tensor product lens here for convenience:

Given any two morphisms
{(f,\tilde{f},b):A\rightarrow X}
and

{(g,\tilde{g},b^{\prime}):B\rightarrow Y}
, we have the morphism

{(f,\tilde{f},b)\otimes(g,\tilde{g},b^{\prime}):A\otimes B\rightarrow X\otimes Y}

defined by

•

the forward map

(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto(f(a_{1}),g(a_{2}))

Equation

17

•

the approximation map

(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto(\tilde{f}(a_{1}),\tilde{g}(a_{2}))

Equation

18

•

the backward map

((a_{1},a_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}))\mapsto(b(a_{1},x_{1}),b^{\prime}(a_{2},x_{2}))

Equation

19

We first check the domain: for all
{(a_{1},a_{2})\in A\otimes B}
and

{(x_{1},x_{2})\in X\otimes Y}
, we assume

d_{X\otimes Y}(\tilde{f}_{\otimes}(a_{1},a_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}))\neq\infty

(6)

and we are required to show

d_{X}(\tilde{f}(a_{1}),x_{1})\neq\infty\text{ and }d_{Y}(\tilde{g}(a_{2}),x_{2})\neq\infty

(7)

which follows directly by assumption.

Given that
Equation

7
holds for all
{(a_{1},a_{2})\in A\otimes B}

and
{(x_{1},x_{2})\in X\otimes Y}
under the assumption given in

Equation

6
, we can freely use Properties 1 and 2 of the lenses

(f,\tilde{f},b)
and
(g,\tilde{g},b^{\prime})
to show that the lens properties
hold for the product:

Property 1.

\displaystyle d_{A\otimes B}\bigl((a_{1},a_{2}),b_{\otimes}((a_{1},a_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\max\bigl(d_{A}(a_{1},b(a_{1},x_{1})),d_{B}(a_{2},b^{\prime}(a_{2},x_{2}))\bigr)

Eq.

19

\displaystyle\leq\max\bigl(d_{X}(\tilde{f}(a_{1}),x_{1}),d_{Y}(\tilde{g}(a_{2}),x_{2})\bigr)

(Property 1 of
(f,\tilde{f},b)
&
(g,\tilde{g},b^{\prime})
)

Property 2.

As above, the property follows directly from
Property 2 of the component
(f,\tilde{f},b)
and
(g,\tilde{g},b^{\prime})
.

Tensor product as bifunctor

Lemma

14
.

The tensor product operation on lenses induces a bifunctor on
Bel
.

Proof.

The functoriality of the triple given in

Equations

17
,
18
and
19
follows by checking
conditions expressing preservation of composition and identities.
Specifically, for any error lenses

h:A\rightarrow B

h^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}

g:B\rightarrow C
and

g^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime}

we must show

(g\otimes g^{\prime})\circ(h\otimes h^{\prime})=(g\circ h)\otimes(g^{\prime}\circ h^{\prime})

We check the backward map:

Given any
(a_{1},a_{2})\in A\otimes A^{\prime}
and
(c_{1},c_{2})\in C\otimes C^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle b_{(g\otimes g^{\prime})\circ(h\otimes h^{\prime})}((a_{1},a_{2}),(c_{1},c_{2}))

\displaystyle=b_{h\otimes h^{\prime}}\bigl((a_{1},a_{2}),b_{g\otimes g^{\prime}}\bigl(\tilde{f}_{h\otimes h^{\prime}}(a_{1},a_{2}),(c_{1},c_{2})\bigr)\bigr)

(8)

\displaystyle=(b_{h}(a_{1},b_{g}(\tilde{f}_{h}(a_{1}),c_{1})),b_{h^{\prime}}(a_{2},b_{g^{\prime}}(\tilde{f}_{h^{\prime}}(a_{2}),c_{2})))

(9)

\displaystyle=b_{(g\circ h)\otimes(g^{\prime}\circ h^{\prime})}((a_{1},a_{2}),(c_{1},c_{2}))

(10)

Moreover, for any objects
X
and
Y
in
Bel
, the identity lenses

id_{X}
and
id_{Y}
clearly satisfy

id_{X}\otimes id_{Y}=id_{X\otimes Y}

∎

Associator

We define the associator

\alpha_{X,Y,Z}:X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)\rightarrow(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z
as the following triple:

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(x,(y,z))

\displaystyle\triangleq((x,y),z)

(11)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(x,(y,z))

\displaystyle\triangleq((x,y),z)

(12)

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}((x,(y,z)),((a,b),c))

\displaystyle\triangleq(a,(b,c)).

(13)

It is straightforward to check that
\alpha_{X,Y,Z}
is an error lens
satisfying Properties 1 and 2. To check that the associator is an isomorphism,
we are required to show the existence of the lens

\alpha^{\prime}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\rightarrow X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)

satisfying

\alpha^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=id_{X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)}

and

\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\circ\alpha^{\prime}=id_{(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z}

where
id
is the identity lens (see
Definition

29
). Defining the forward
and approximation maps for
\alpha^{\prime}
is straightforward; for the forward map we
have

\displaystyle f_{\alpha^{\prime}}((x,y),z)

\displaystyle\triangleq(x,(y,z))

and the approximation map is defined identically. For the backward map we have

\displaystyle b_{\alpha^{\prime}}\bigl(((x,y),z),(a,(b,c))\bigr)\triangleq((a,b),c)

It is straightforward to check that
\alpha^{\prime}
satisfies Properties 1 and 2 of
an error lens.

The naturality of the associator follows by checking that the following diagram
commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X,Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{X}\otimes(g_{Y}\otimes g_{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X,Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{X}\otimes g_{Y})\otimes g_{Z}}

That is, we check that

((g_{X}\otimes g_{Y})\otimes g_{Z})\circ\alpha_{X,Y,Z}=\alpha_{X,Y,Z}\circ(g_{X}\otimes(g_{Y}\otimes g_{Z}))

for the error lenses

\displaystyle g_{X}

\displaystyle:X\rightarrow X\triangleq(f_{X},\tilde{f}_{X},b_{X})

\displaystyle g_{Y}

\displaystyle:Y\rightarrow Y\triangleq(f_{Y},\tilde{f}_{Y},b_{Y})

\displaystyle g_{Z}

\displaystyle:Z\rightarrow Z\triangleq(f_{Z},\tilde{f}_{Z},b_{Z})

This follows from the definitions of lens composition (
Definition

28
)
and the tensor product on lenses
(
Eqs.

17
,
18
and
19
).
We detail here the case of the backward map.

Using the notation
b_{g}
(resp.
\tilde{f}_{g}
) to refer to both of the
backward maps (resp. approximation maps) of the tensor product lenses of
the lenses
g_{X}
,
g_{Y}
, and
g_{Z}
, we are required to show that

b_{\alpha}\left(xyz,b_{g}\left(\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(xyz),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)=b_{g}\left(xyz,b_{\alpha}\left(\tilde{f}_{g}(xyz),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)

(14)

for any
xyz\in X\ \otimes\ (Y\otimes Z)
and

x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\in(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z
:

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}\left(xyz,b_{g}\left(\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(xyz),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=b_{g}\left(xyz,b_{\alpha}\left(\tilde{f}_{g}(xyz),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}\left(xyz,b_{g}\left(\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(xyz),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=b_{g}\left(xyz,(x^{\prime},(y^{\prime},z^{\prime}))\right)

by
Equation

13

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}\left(xyz,b_{g}\left(((x,y),z),x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=b_{g}\left(xyz,(x^{\prime},(y^{\prime},z^{\prime}))\right)

by
Equation

12

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}\left(xyz,((b_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),b_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})),b_{Z}(z,z^{\prime}))\right)

\displaystyle=(b_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),(b_{Y}(y,y^{\prime}),b_{Z}(z,z^{\prime})))

by
Equation

19

\displaystyle(b_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),(b_{Y}(y,y^{\prime}),b_{Z}(z,z^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=(b_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),(b_{Y}(y,y^{\prime}),b_{Z}(z,z^{\prime})))

by
Equation

13

Unitors

We define the left-unitor

\lambda_{X}:I\otimes X\rightarrow X
as

\displaystyle f_{\lambda}(\star,x)

\displaystyle\triangleq x

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\lambda}(\star,x)

\displaystyle\triangleq x

\displaystyle b_{\lambda}((\star,x),x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq(\star,x^{\prime})

The right-unitor is similarly defined.

The fact that
d_{I}(\star,\star)=-\infty
is essential in order for

\lambda_{X}
to satisfy the first property of an error lens:

\displaystyle d_{I\otimes X}(x,b_{\lambda}((\star,x),x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(\tilde{f}_{\lambda}(\star,x),x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\max{(-\infty,d_{X}(x,x^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})

Checking the naturality of
\lambda_{X}
amounts to checking that
the following diagram commutes for all error lenses

g:X\rightarrow Y
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\otimes X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\otimes Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{I}\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Symmetry

We define the symmetry map
\gamma_{X,Y}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow Y\otimes X
as the following triple:

\displaystyle f_{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\triangleq(y,x)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\triangleq(y,x)

\displaystyle b_{\gamma}((x,y),(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\triangleq(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

It is straightforward to check that
\gamma_{X,Y}
is an error
lens. Checking the naturality of
\gamma_{X,Y}
amounts to
checking that the following diagram commutes for any error
lenses
g_{1}:X\rightarrow Y
and
g_{2}:Y\rightarrow X
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\otimes Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\otimes X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\otimes X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\otimes Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}\otimes g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{Y,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}\otimes g_{1}}

2
Coproducts

Property 1

For any
x\in X
and
z\in X+Y
,

d_{X}(x,b_{in_{1}}(x,z))\leq d_{X+Y}(\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}(x),z)

supposing

{d_{X+Y}(\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}(x),z)={d_{X+Y}(inl\ x,z)\neq\infty}}.

From
{d_{X+Y}(inl\ x,z)\neq\infty}
and
Equation

20
, we know

z=inl\ x_{0}
for some
x_{0}\in X
, and so we must show

d_{X}(x,x_{0})\leq d_{X+Y}(inl\ x,inl\ x_{0})

which follows from
Equation

20
and reflexivity.

Property 2

For any
x\in X
and
z\in X+Y
,

{f_{in_{1}}(b_{in_{1}}(x,z))=z}
supposing

d_{X+Y}(\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}(x),z)={d_{X+Y}(inl\ x,z)\neq\infty}

From
{d_{X+Y}(inl\ x,z)\neq\infty}
and
Equation

20
, we know

z=inl\ x_{0}
for some
x_{0}\in X
, and so we must show

f_{in_{1}}(x_{0})=inl\ x_{0}

which follows from
Equation

21
.

Property 1

For all
z\in X+Y
and
c\in C
,

d_{X+Y}\left(z,b_{[g,h]}(z,c)\right)\leq d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{[g,h]}(z),c\right)

supposing

d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{[g,h]}(z),c\right)\neq\infty.

This follows directly given that
g
and
h
are error lenses:

If
z=inl\ x
for some
x\in X
then

d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{g}(x),c\right)\neq\infty
and we use Property 1
for
g
to satisfy the desired conclusion:

{d_{X}(x,(b_{g}(x,c)))\leq d_{C}(\tilde{f}_{g}(x),c)}
. Otherwise,
z=inr\ y

for some
y\in Y
then
d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{h}(y),c\right)\neq\infty

and we use Property 1 for
h
.

Property 2

For all
z\in X+Y
and
c\in C
,

f_{[g,h]}\left(b_{[g,h]}(z,c)\right)=c

supposing
d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{[g,h]}(z),c\right)\neq\infty
.
If
z=inl\ x
for some
x\in X
then

d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{g}(x),c\right)\neq\infty

and we use Property 2 for
g
. Otherwise,
z=inr\ y
for some
y\in Y

then
d_{C}\left(\tilde{f}_{h}(y),c\right)\neq\infty
and we use
Property 2 for
h
.

To show that
[g,h]\circ in_{1}=g
(resp.
[g,h]\circ in_{2}=h
),
we observe that the following diagrams, by definition, commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{in_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{[g,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{g}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{[g,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{g}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X+Y)\times C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}\times id_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{[g,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{in_{1}}}

Uniqueness of the copairing

We check the uniqueness of copairing by showing that for any two morphisms

g_{1}:X\rightarrow C
and
g_{2}:Y\rightarrow C
, if

h\circ in_{1}=g_{1}
and
h\circ in_{2}=g_{2}
for any

h:X+Y\rightarrow C
, then
h=[g_{1},g_{2}]
.

We detail the cases for the forward and backward map; the case for the
approximation map is identical to that of the forward map.

forward map

We are required to show that

f_{h}(z)=f_{[g_{1},g_{2}]}(z)
for any
{z\in X+Y}
assuming that

f_{in_{1}};f_{h}=f_{g_{1}}
and
f_{in_{2}};f_{h}=f_{g_{2}}
.
The desired conclusion follows by cases on
z
; i.e.,
z=inl\ x

for some
x\in X
or
z=inr\ y
for some
y\in Y
.

backward map

We are required to show that

b_{h}(z,c)=b_{[g_{1},g_{2}]}(z,c)
for any
z\in X+Y
and
c\in C
.
Unfolding definitions in the assumptions
b_{h\circ in_{1}}=b_{g_{1}}

and
b_{h\circ in_{2}}=b_{g_{2}}
, we have that

b_{in_{1}}(x,b_{h}\left(\tilde{f}_{in_{1}}(x),c_{1}\right))=b_{g_{1}}(x,c_{1})

for any
x\in X
and
c_{1}\in C
and

b_{in_{2}}(y,b_{h}\left(\tilde{f}_{in_{2}}(y),c_{2}\right))=b_{g_{2}}(y,c_{2})

for any
y\in Y
and
c_{2}\in C
. We proceed by cases on
z
.

If
z=inl\ x
for some
x\in X
then we are required to show that

b_{h}(inl\ x,c)=inl\ (b_{g_{1}}(x,c)).

By definition of lens
composition, we have that

d_{X+Y}\left(inl\ x,b_{h}(inl\ x,c)\right)\neq\infty,

so

b_{h}(inl\ x,c)=inl\ x_{0}
for some
x_{0}\in X
. By assumption, we
then have that
b_{g_{1}}(x,c)=b_{in_{1}}(x,inl\ x_{0})=x_{0}
, from which
the desired conclusion follows.

The case of
z=inr\ y
for some
y\in Y
is identical.

Appendix 6.C
Interpreting
Bean
Terms

In this section of the appendix, we detail the constructions for
interpreting
Bean
terms (
).

Applications of the symmetry map
s_{X,Y}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times X

and 2-monoidality
m_{r,A,B}:D_{R}(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{r}(A\otimes B)

are often elided for succinctness. Recall the discrete diagonal
t_{X}:X\rightarrow X\times X
(
Lemma

15
), which will be used frequently in the
following constructions.

Case (Var).

Suppose that
\Gamma=x_{0}:_{q_{0}}\sigma_{0},\dots,x_{i-1}:_{q_{i-1}}\sigma_{i-1}
. Define the map

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma\vdash x:\sigma}\rrbracket

as the composition

\pi_{i}\circ(\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\sigma_{0}}\rrbracket}\otimes\cdots\otimes\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\sigma_{i-1}}\rrbracket}\otimes\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket})\circ(m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\sigma_{0}}\rrbracket}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\sigma_{i-1}}\rrbracket}\otimes m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}),

where the lens
\pi_{i}
is the
i
th projection. Note that all types
\sigma
and

\sigma_{j}
are interpreted as metric spaces, i.e., satisfying reflexivity.

Case (DVar).

Define the map

\llbracket{\Phi,z:\alpha\mid\Gamma\vdash x:\alpha}\rrbracket

as the
i
th projection lens
\pi_{i}
, assuming

\Phi=z_{0}:\alpha_{0},\dots,z_{i-1}:\alpha_{i-1}
. Note that all discrete
types
\alpha_{j}
and
\alpha
are interpreted as discrete metric spaces, i.e.,
with self-distance zero.

Case (Unit).

Define the map

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash():\text{unit}}\rrbracket

as the lens
\mathcal{L}_{unit}
from a tuple

\bar{x}\in\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma}\rrbracket
to the singleton
of the carrier in
I=(\{\star\},\underline{0})
, defined as

\displaystyle f_{unit}(\bar{x})

\displaystyle\triangleq\star

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{unit}(\bar{x})

\displaystyle\triangleq\star

\displaystyle b_{unit}(\bar{x},\star)

\displaystyle\triangleq\bar{x}.

We verify that the triple
\mathcal{L}_{unit}
is an error lens.

Property 1.

For any
\bar{x}\in X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{i}
we must show

\displaystyle d_{X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{i}}\left(\bar{x},b_{c}(\bar{x},\star)\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{I}\left(\tilde{f}_{unit}(\bar{x}),\star\right)

\displaystyle\max(d_{X_{1}}(x_{1},x_{1}),\cdots,d_{X_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq 0,

which holds under the assumption that all types are interpreted as metric spaces
with negative self distance.

Property 2.

For any
\bar{x}\in X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{i}
we have

f_{unit}\left(b_{unit}(\bar{x},\star)\right)=f_{unit}(\bar{x})=\star.

Case (
\otimes
I).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Delta\vdash f:\tau}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket

define the map

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau}\rrbracket

as the composition

(h_{1}\otimes h_{2})\circ(t_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Gamma,\Delta}\rrbracket}),

where the map
t_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket
is
the diagonal lens on discrete metric spaces (
Lemma

15
).

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}}\rrbracket}

(15)

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Delta,x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2}\vdash f:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\otimes D_{r}\llbracket{\tau_{1}}\rrbracket\otimes D_{r}\llbracket{\tau_{2}}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}

(16)

we must define a

\llbracket{\Phi\mid r+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrbracket
.
We first define a map

h:D_{r}\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes D_{r}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket

as the composition

h_{2}\circ((m^{-1}_{r,\llbracket{\tau_{1}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\tau_{2}}\rrbracket}\circ D_{r}(h_{1}))\otimes(\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\circ m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket})\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})\circ(t_{D_{r}\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\otimes id_{D_{r}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket}).

Now, observing that
\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket
is a discrete space, the set maps from the lens
h

define the desired lens:

\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes D_{r}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket

Case (
\otimes
E
α
).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}}\rrbracket}

(17)

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi,x:\alpha_{1},y:\alpha_{2};\Delta\vdash f:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\alpha_{1}}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\alpha_{2}}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}

(18)

define the map

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrbracket

as the composition

h_{2}\circ(h_{1}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})\circ(t_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket}).

Case (
+
E).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:\sigma+\tau}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma+\tau}\rrbracket

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Delta,x:_{q}\sigma\vdash e:\rho}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\otimes D_{q}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Delta,y:_{q}\tau\vdash f:\rho}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\otimes D_{q}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket

we require a lens

\llbracket{\Phi\mid q+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}:\rho}\rrbracket
.
We first define a lens

h:D_{q}\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes D_{q}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket

as the composition

[h_{2},h_{3}]\circ\Theta\circ((\eta\circ D_{q}(h_{1}))\otimes(\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\circ m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket})\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})\circ(t_{D_{q}{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}}\otimes id_{D_{q}\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})

Now, observing that
\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket
is a discrete space, the set maps from the lens
h

define the desired lens. Above, the map

\eta:D_{q}\llbracket{\sigma+\tau}\rrbracket\rightarrow D_{q}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket+D_{q}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket

is the identity lens, and the map

\Theta_{X,Y,Z}:X\otimes(Y+Z)\rightarrow(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)

is given by the triple

\displaystyle f_{\Theta}(x,w)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}inl\ (x,y)&\text{if }w=inl\ y\\
inr\ (x,z)&\text{if }w=inr\ z\end{cases}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\Theta}(x,w)

\displaystyle\triangleq f_{\Theta}(x,w)

\displaystyle b_{\Theta}((x,w),u)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}(\pi_{1}a,inl\ (\pi_{2}a))&\text{if }u=inl\ a\\
(\pi_{1}a,inr\ (\pi_{2}a))&\text{if }u=inr\ a\\
(x,w)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We check that the triple

\Theta_{X,Y,Z}:X\otimes(Y+Z)\rightarrow(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)\triangleq(f_{\Theta},\tilde{f}_{\Theta},b_{\Theta})

is well-defined.

Property 1.

For any
x\in X
,
w\in Y+Z
, and

u\in(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)
we are required to show

\displaystyle d_{X\otimes(Y+Z)}\left((x,w),b_{\Theta}((x,w),u)\right)\leq d_{(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)}\left(\tilde{f}_{\Theta}(x,w),u\right)

(19)

supposing

d_{(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)}\left(\tilde{f}_{\Theta}(x,w),u\right)\neq\infty.

(20)

From
Equation

20
, and by unfolding definitions, we have

(a)

if
w=inl\ y
for some
y\in Y
, then
u=inl\ (x_{1},y_{1})

for some
(x_{1},y_{1})\in X\otimes Y

(b)

if
w=inr\ z
for some
z\in Z
, then
u=inr\ (x_{1},z_{1})

for some
(x_{1},z_{1})\in X\otimes Z
.

In both cases, the
Equation

19
is an equality.

Property 2.

For any
x\in X
,
w\in Y+Z
, and

u\in(X\otimes Y)+(X\otimes Z)
we are required to show

{f_{\Theta}(b_{\Theta}((x,w),u))=u}

(21)

supposing
Equation

20
holds.

We consider the cases when
u=inl\ (x_{1},y_{1})
for some

(x_{1},y_{1})\in X\otimes Y
and when

u=inr\ (x_{1},z_{1})
for some
(x_{1},z_{1})\in X\otimes Z

as we did for Property 1

In the first case, we have

\displaystyle f_{\Theta}(b_{\Theta}((x,w),u))

\displaystyle=f_{\Theta}(x_{1},inl\ y_{1})

\displaystyle=inl\ (x_{1},y_{1}).

In the second case we have

\displaystyle f_{\Theta}(b_{\Theta}((x,w),u))

\displaystyle=f_{\Theta}(x_{1},inr\ z_{1})

\displaystyle=inr\ (x_{1},z_{1}).

Case (
+\ \text{I}_{L,R}
).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{l}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket

\displaystyle h_{r}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket

define the maps

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash\textbf{inl }e:\sigma+\tau}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq in_{1}\circ h_{l}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash\textbf{inr }e:\sigma+\tau}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\triangleq in_{2}\circ h_{r}.

Case (Let).

See
Section

3
.

Case (Disc).

Given the lens

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\text{num}}\rrbracket
from the premise,
we can define the map
\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\text{dnum}}\rrbracket

directly by verifying the lens conditions.

Case (DLet).

Given the maps

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\alpha}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\alpha}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Phi,x:\alpha\mid\Delta\vdash f:\sigma}\rrbracket:\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\alpha}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket\rightarrow{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}

define the map

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrbracket
as the composition

h_{2}\circ(h_{1}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})\circ(t_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Delta}\rrbracket})

Case (Add).

See
Section

3
.

Case (Sub).

We proceed the same as the case for (Add). We define a lens

\mathcal{L}_{sub}:D_{\varepsilon}(R)\otimes D_{\varepsilon}(R)\rightarrow R

given by the triple

\displaystyle f_{sub}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq x_{1}-x_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{sub}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq(x_{1}-x_{2})e^{\delta};\quad|\delta|\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle b_{sub}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\frac{x_{3}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{2}},\frac{x_{3}x_{2}}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right).

We check that
\mathcal{L}_{sub}:D_{\varepsilon}(R)\otimes D_{\varepsilon}(R)\rightarrow R
is well-defined.

For any
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in R
such that

d_{R}\left(\tilde{f}_{sub}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}\right)\neq\infty.

(22)

holds, we need to check that
\mathcal{L}_{sub}
satisfies the properties of an
error lens. We take the distance function
d_{R}
as the metric given in

Equation

7
, so
Equation

22
implies that
(x_{1}-x_{2})
and
x_{3}

are either both zero or are both non-zero and of the same sign.

Property 1.

We are required to show that

\displaystyle d_{R\otimes R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{sub}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\left(\tilde{f}_{sub}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\left((x_{1}-x_{2})e^{\delta},x_{3}\right).

Without loss of generality, we consider the case when

d_{R\otimes R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{sub}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)=d_{R}\left(x_{1},\frac{x_{3}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right);

that is,

d_{R}\left(x_{2},\frac{x_{3}x_{2}}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right)\leq d_{R}\left(x_{1},\frac{x_{3}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right).

Unfolding the definition of the distance function given in
Equation

7
,
we are required to show

\left|{\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{x_{3}}\right)}\right|\leq\left|{\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}-x_{2}}{x_{3}}\right)}+\delta\right|+\varepsilon.

(23)

which holds under the assumptions of
|\delta|\leq\varepsilon
and

0<\varepsilon
; the proof is identical to that given for the case of the
Add rule.

Property 2.

\displaystyle f_{sub}\left(b_{sub}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)=f_{sub}\left(\frac{x_{3}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{2}},\frac{x_{3}x_{2}}{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right)=x_{3}.

Case (Mul).

See
Section

3
.

Case (Div).

We proceed the same as the case for (Add), with slightly different indices.
We define a lens
\mathcal{L}_{div}:D_{\varepsilon/2}(R)\otimes D_{\varepsilon/2}(R)\rightarrow(R+\diamond)
given by the triple

\displaystyle f_{div}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}{x_{1}}/{x_{2}}&\text{ if }x_{2}\neq 0\\
\diamond&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{div}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}{x_{1}}e^{\delta}/{x_{2}}&\text{ if }x_{2}\neq 0;\quad|\delta|\leq\varepsilon\\
\diamond&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle b_{div}((x_{1},x_{2}),x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\left(\sqrt{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}},\sqrt{{x_{1}x_{2}}/x_{3}}\right)&\text{ if }x=inl\ x_{3}\\
(x_{1},x_{2})&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

We check that
\mathcal{L}_{div}:D_{\varepsilon/2}(R)\otimes D_{\varepsilon/2}(R)\rightarrow(R+\diamond)
is well-defined.

For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in R
and
x\in R+\diamond
such that

d_{R+\diamond}\left(\tilde{f}_{div}(x_{1},x_{2}),x\right)\neq\infty.

(24)

holds, we are required to show that
\mathcal{L}_{div}
satisfies the
properties of an error lens. From
Equation

24
and again assuming the
distance function is given by
Equation

7
, we know
x=inl\ x_{3}
for some

x_{3}\in R
,
x_{2}\neq 0
, and
x_{1}/x_{2}
and
x_{3}
are either both zero or both
non-zero and of the same sign; this guarantees that the backward map
(containing square roots) is indeed well defined.

Property 1.

We need to show

\displaystyle d_{R\otimes R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{div}((x_{1},x_{2}),x)\right)-\varepsilon/2

\displaystyle\leq d_{R+\diamond}\left(\tilde{f}_{div}(x_{1},x_{2}),x\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}e^{\delta},x_{3}\right).

(25)

Unfolding the definition of the distance function (
Equation

7
), we have

\displaystyle d_{R\otimes R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{div}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=d_{R}\left(x_{1},x_{1}\sqrt{{x_{1}}{x_{2}x_{3}}}\right)

\displaystyle=d_{R}\left(x_{2},x_{2}\sqrt{{x_{1}x_{2}}/{x_{3}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}x_{3}}\right)\right|,

and so we are required to show

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}x_{3}}\right)\right|\leq\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}x_{3}}\right)+\delta\right|+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon,

which holds under the assumptions of
|\delta|\leq\varepsilon
and
0<\varepsilon
; the proof is identical to that given for the case of the Mul rule.

Property 2.

\displaystyle f_{div}\left(b_{div}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)=f_{div}\left(\sqrt{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}},\sqrt{{x_{1}x_{2}}/x_{3}}\right)=x_{3}.

Case (DMul).

We proceed similarly as for (Add). We define a
lens
\mathcal{L}_{dmul}:(R^{\alpha}\otimes D_{\varepsilon}R)\rightarrow R

given by the triple

\displaystyle f_{dmul}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq x_{1}x_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{dmul}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\triangleq x_{1}x_{2}e^{\delta};\quad|\delta|\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle b_{dmul}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})

\displaystyle\triangleq(x_{1},x_{3}/x_{1}).

We check that

\mathcal{L}_{dmul}:R^{\alpha}\otimes D_{\varepsilon}R\rightarrow R
is well-defined.

For any
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in R
such that

d_{R}\left(\tilde{f}_{dmul}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}\right)\neq\infty.

(26)

holds, we need to check the that
\mathcal{L}_{dmul}
satisfies the properties
of an error lens. We again take the distance function
d_{R}
as the metric
given in
Equation

7
, so
Equation

26
implies that
(x_{1}x_{2})

and
x_{3}
are either both zero or are both non-zero and of the same sign;
this guarantees that the backward map (containing square roots) is indeed
well defined.

Property 1.

We are required to show

\displaystyle d_{R^{\alpha}\otimes D_{\varepsilon}R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{dmul}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\left(\tilde{f}_{dmul}(x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\left(x_{1}x_{2}e^{\delta},x_{3}\right)

Unfolding the definition of the distance function (
Equation

7
), we have

\displaystyle d_{R^{\alpha}\otimes D_{\varepsilon}R}\left((x_{1},x_{2}),b_{dmul}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)

\displaystyle=\max\left(d_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{1}),d_{R}(x_{2},x_{3}/x_{1})-\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{x_{3}}\right)\right|-\varepsilon,

and so we are required to show

\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{x_{3}}\right)\right|\leq\left|\ln\left(\frac{x_{1}x_{2}}{x_{3}}\right)+\delta\right|+\varepsilon

(27)

which holds under the assumptions of
|\delta|\leq\varepsilon
and
0<\varepsilon
;
the proof is identical to that given in the Add rule.

Property 2.

\displaystyle f_{dmul}\left(b_{dmul}((x_{1},x_{2}),x_{3})\right)=f_{dmul}\left(x_{1},x_{3}/x_{1}\right)=x_{3}.

Appendix 6.D
Interpreting
\Lambda_{S}
Terms

This appendix provides the detailed constructions of the interpretation of

\Lambda_{S}
terms for
Definition

32
. The interpretation of terms is defined
over the typing derivations for
\Lambda_{S}
given in

Figure

4
. For each case, the ideal interpretation

{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{id}
is constructed explicitly, but the construction for

{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}
is nearly identical, requiring only that the forgetful functor

U_{ap}
is used in place of
U_{id}
.

Applications of the symmetry map
s_{X,Y}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times X

are elided for succinctness. The diagonal map
d_{X}:X\rightarrow X\times X

on
Set
is used frequently and is not elided.

Case (Var).

Define the maps

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta\vdash x:\sigma}\rrparenthesis_{id}
and

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta\vdash x:\sigma}\rrparenthesis_{ap}

in
Set
as the appropriate projection
\pi_{i}
.

Define the set maps

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash():\text{unit}}\rrparenthesis_{id}
and

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash():\text{unit}}\rrparenthesis_{ap}
as the constant function
returning the value
\star
.

Case (Const).

Define the maps

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash k:\text{num}}\rrparenthesis_{id}
and

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash k:\text{num}}\rrparenthesis_{ap}

in
Set
as the constant function taking points in

\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma}\rrparenthesis
to the value
k\in R
.

Appendix 6.E
Details for Soundness

This appendix provides details for the proofs in
Section

4
, which
presents the main backward error soundness theorem for
Bean
. A detailed
proof of the main theorem is provided in
Appendix

6.F
.
In this appendix, we provide the details for auxiliary results.
The full typing relation for
\Lambda_{S}
is defined by the rules in

Figure

4
, and the operational semantics for
\Lambda_{S}
are
given in
Figure

5
.

Lemma

16
.

Let
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
be a well-typed term in
Bean
.
Then there is a derivation of
\Phi,\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash e:\tau
in
\Lambda_{S}
.

Proof.

The proof of
Lemma

16
follows by induction on the
Bean
derivation

\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
. Most cases are immediate by application of
the corresponding
\Lambda_{S}
rule. The rules for primitive operations require
application of the
\Lambda_{S}
(Var) rule. We demonstrate the derivation for the
case of the (Add) rule:

Case (Add).

Given a
Bean
derivation of

\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon+r_{1}}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon+r_{2}}\text{num}\vdash\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}:\text{num}

we are required to show a
\Lambda_{S}
derivation of

\Phi,\Gamma,x:\text{num},y:\text{num}\vdash\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}:\text{num}

which follows by application of the Var rule for
\Lambda_{S}
:

(Var)

\Phi,\Gamma,x:\text{num}\vdash x:\text{num}

(Var)

y:\text{num}\vdash y:\text{num}

(Add)

\Phi,\Gamma,x:\text{num},y:\text{num}\vdash\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}:\text{num}

∎

Lemma

18
.

Let
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
be a
Bean
program. Then we have

U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{ap}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}.

Proof.

The proof of
Lemma

18
follows by induction on the structure of the

Bean
derivation
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
. We detail here the
cases of pairing for the ideal semantics.

U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

Case (Var).

\displaystyle U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma\vdash x:\sigma}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=U_{id}(\varepsilon_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\circ m_{0\leq r,\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\circ\pi_{i})

(
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}

(Definition of
U_{id}
)

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma^{\circ},x:\sigma\vdash x:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(
Definition

32
)

Case (DVar).

\displaystyle U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi,z:\alpha\mid\Gamma\vdash z:\sigma}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}

(
)

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,x:\sigma,\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash x:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(
Definition

32
)

Case (Unit).

\displaystyle U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash():\text{unit}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=f_{unit}

(
)

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash():\text{unit}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(
Definition

32
)

Case (
\otimes
I).

From the induction hypothesis we have

\displaystyle U_{id}{(h_{1})}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle U_{id}{(h_{2})}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Delta\vdash f:\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

We conclude as follows:

\displaystyle U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=U_{id}(t_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}\otimes id_{\llbracket{\Gamma\otimes\Delta}\rrbracket});U_{id}(h_{1}\otimes h_{2})

(
)

\displaystyle=(t_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});(U_{id}(h_{1}),U_{id}(h_{2}))

(Definition of
U_{id}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(IH &
Definition

32
)

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

From the induction hypothesis we have

\displaystyle U_{id}{(h_{1})}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma^{\circ}\vdash e:\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle U_{id}{(h_{2})}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Delta^{\circ},x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2}\vdash f:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

We conclude with the following:

\displaystyle U_{id}\llbracket{\Phi\mid r+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrbracket

(Definition of

Substitution

Theorem

6
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
be a well-typed
\Lambda_{S}
term. Then for any
well-typed substitution
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Gamma
of closed values, there
is a derivation
\emptyset\vdash e[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Gamma)]:\tau.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the derivation
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
. The
cases for (Var), (Unit), (Const), and (+ I) are trivial;
\Lambda_{S}
is a simple
first-order language and the remaining cases are routine.

Case (
\otimes
I).

We have a well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
and it is straightforward to show that the induction
hypothesis yields the premises needed for applying the typing rule (
\otimes

I). The desired conclusion then follows from the definition of substitution.

Case (
\otimes
E).

We are required to show

\emptyset\vdash(\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f})[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)]:\sigma

given the well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
. From
\bar{\gamma}
we derive a substitution

\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma
, and from the induction hypothesis on the
left premise we have
\emptyset\vdash e[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma)]:\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}
; by inversion on this hypothesis, we derive a
substitution which allows us to use the induction hypothesis for the right
premise. This provides the premises needed to apply the typing rule (
\otimes

E). The desired conclusion then follows from the definition of substitution.

Case (+ E).

We are required to show

\emptyset\vdash(\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.e$})})[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)]:\rho

given the well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
. From
\bar{\gamma}
we derive a substitution

\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma
, and from the induction hypothesis on the
left premise we have
\emptyset\vdash e^{\prime}[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma)]:\sigma+\tau
; we first apply inversion to this hypothesis and then reason by
cases to derive a substitution which allows us to use the induction hypothesis
for the right premise. This provides the premises needed to apply the typing
rule (+ E). The desired conclusion then follows from the definition of
substitution.

Case (Let).

We are required to show

\emptyset\vdash(\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f})[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)]:\sigma

given a well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vdash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
. From
\bar{\gamma}
we derive a substitution

\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma
, and from the induction hypothesis on the
left premise we have
\emptyset\vdash e[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma)]
; by
inversion on this hypothesis, we derive a substitution which allows us to
use the induction hypothesis for the right premise. This provides the premises
needed to apply the typing rule (Let). The desired conclusion then follows
from the definition of substitution.

Case (Op).

We have a well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
and it is straightforward to show that the induction
hypothesis yields the premises needed for applying the typing rule (Op).
The desired conclusion then follows from the definition of substitution.

Case (Div).

We have a well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)
and it is straightforward to show that the induction
hypothesis yields the premises needed for applying the typing rule (Div).
The desired conclusion then follows from the definition of substitution.

∎

Soundness of
{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

In this section of the appendix, we prove the soundness of our denotational
semantics. Namely, we show that our interpretation of
\Lambda_{S}

(
Definition

32
) respects the operational semantics given in

Figure

5
.

Applications of the symmetry map
s_{X,Y}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times X

are elided for succinctness. Recall the diagonal map
d_{X}:X\rightarrow X\times X
on
Set
, which is used frequently in the interpretation of

\Lambda_{S}
.

Theorem

8
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
be a well-typed
\Lambda_{S}
term. Then for any
well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Gamma
, if

e[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Gamma)]\Downarrow_{id}v
for some value
v
, then

{\llparenthesis{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}\rrparenthesis_{id}\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis_{id}=\llparenthesis{v}\rrparenthesis_{id}}
(and similarly for
\Downarrow_{ap}
and
{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}
).

Proof.

By induction on the structure of the
\Lambda_{S}
derivations
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
. The cases for (Var), (Unit), (Const), and (+ I) are trivial. In each
case we apply inversion on the step relation to obtain the premise for the
induction hypothesis.

Case (
\otimes
I).

We are required to show

{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}={\llparenthesis{(u,v)}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

for some well-typed closed substitution

\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta

and value
(u,v)
such that

(e,f)[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)]\Downarrow_{id}(u,v)

From
\bar{\gamma}
we derive the substitutions
\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\Phi
,

\bar{\gamma}_{1}\vDash\Gamma
, and
\bar{\gamma}_{2}\vDash\Delta
.
By inversion on the step relation we then have

\displaystyle e[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}_{1}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma)]

\displaystyle\Downarrow_{id}u

\displaystyle f[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}_{2}/dom(\Phi,\Delta)]

\displaystyle\Downarrow_{id}v

We conclude as follows:

\displaystyle{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle=\bigl((d_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});({\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id},{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Delta\vdash f:\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id})\bigr){\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle=\bigl({\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id},{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Delta\vdash f:\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}\bigr)\bigl(\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{1}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{2}}\rrparenthesis\bigr)

\displaystyle=({\llparenthesis{u}\rrparenthesis}_{id},{\llparenthesis{v}\rrparenthesis}_{id})

(IH)

Case (
\otimes
E).

We are required to show

{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}={\llparenthesis{w}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

for some well-typed closed substitution
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta
and value
w
such that

(\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f})[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta)]\Downarrow_{id}w

From
\bar{\gamma}
we derive the substitutions

\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\vDash\Phi
,
\bar{\gamma}_{1}\vDash\Gamma
,
and
\bar{\gamma}_{2}\vDash\Delta
. By inversion on the step
relation we then have

\displaystyle e[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}_{1}/dom(\Phi,\Gamma)]

\displaystyle\Downarrow_{id}(u,v)

\displaystyle f[\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}_{2}/dom(\Phi,\Delta)][u/x][v/y]

\displaystyle\Downarrow_{id}w

We conclude as follows:

\displaystyle{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle=\bigl((d_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});(h_{1},id_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});h_{2}\bigr){\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(
Definition

32
)

\displaystyle=\bigl((h_{1},id_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});h_{2}\bigr)\bigl(\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{1}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{2}}\rrparenthesis\bigr)

(Definition of
d_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis}
)

\displaystyle=\bigl({\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Delta,x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2}\vdash f:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}\bigr)\bigl(\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{2}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{u}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{v}\rrparenthesis\bigr)

(IH)

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis{w}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

(IH)

Case (
+
E).

We are required to show

{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}:\rho}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}={\llparenthesis{w}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

for some well-typed closed substitution
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta
and value

\displaystyle{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}:\rho}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle=\bigl((d_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis},id_{\llparenthesis{\Gamma}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});(h_{1},id_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});\Theta^{S}_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\sigma}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\tau}\rrparenthesis};[h_{2},h_{3}]\bigr){\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

\displaystyle=\bigl((h_{1},id_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis});\Theta^{S}_{\llparenthesis{\Phi}\rrparenthesis\times\llparenthesis{\Delta}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\sigma}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\tau}\rrparenthesis};[h_{2},h_{3}]\bigr)\bigl(\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{1}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}}\rrparenthesis,\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}_{2}}\rrparenthesis\bigr)

Adequacy of
{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

In this section of the appendix, we prove the computational adequacy of our
denotational semantics. Namely, we show that if two
\Lambda_{S}
terms are equal
under our denotational semantics (
Definition

32
), then they will evaluate
to the same value under our operational semantics given in

Figure

5
.

Theorem

9
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash e:\tau
be a well-typed
\Lambda_{S}
term. Then for any
well-typed substitution of closed values
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Gamma
, if

\llparenthesis{\Gamma\vdash e:\tau}\rrparenthesis_{id}\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis_{id}=\llparenthesis{v}\rrparenthesis_{id}
for some value
v
, then
e[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Gamma)]\Downarrow_{id}v
(and similarly for
\Downarrow_{ap}
and
{\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}
).

Proof.

The proof follows directly by cases on
e
. Many cases are immediate and the
remaining cases, given that
\Lambda_{S}
is deterministic, follow by substitution
(
Theorem

6
) and normalization (
Theorem

7
). We show two
representative cases.

Case.

Given
\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta\vdash x:\sigma
and
{\llparenthesis{\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta\vdash x:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}={\llparenthesis{v}\rrparenthesis}_{id}
for
some value
v
and some well-typed substitution
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta
we are required to show

x[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Gamma,x:\sigma,\Delta)]\Downarrow_{id}v

which follows by substitution (
Theorem

6
) and normalization
(
Theorem

7
).

Case.

Given
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\tau
and
{\llparenthesis{\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\tau}\rrparenthesis}_{id}{\llparenthesis{\bar{\gamma}}\rrparenthesis}_{id}={\llparenthesis{w}\rrparenthesis}_{id}
for some
value
w
and some well-typed derivation
\bar{\gamma}\vDash\Gamma,\Delta

we are required to show

(\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f})[\bar{\gamma}/dom(\Gamma,\Delta)]\Downarrow_{id}w

which follows by substitution (
Theorem

6
) and normalization
(
Theorem

7
).

∎

Appendix 6.F
Proof of Backward Error Soundness

This appendix provides a detailed proof of the main backward error soundness
theorem for
Bean
(
Theorem

10
).

Theorem

10
.

Let
\Phi\mid x_{1}:_{r_{1}}\sigma_{1},\cdots,x_{n}:_{r_{n}}\sigma_{n}=\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
be a well-typed
Bean
term. Then for
any well-typed substitutions

\bar{p}\vDash\Phi
and
\bar{k}\vDash\Gamma^{\circ}
, if

e[\bar{p}/dom(\Phi)][\bar{k}/dom(\Gamma)]\Downarrow_{ap}v

for some value
v
, then the well-typed substitution
\bar{l}\vDash\Gamma^{\circ}

exists such that

e[\bar{p}/dom(\Phi)][\bar{l}/dom(\Gamma)]\Downarrow_{id}v,

and

d_{\llbracket{\sigma_{i}}\rrbracket}({k}_{i},{l}_{i})\leq r_{i}
for each
k_{i}\in\bar{k}

and
l_{i}\in\bar{l}
.

Proof.

From the lens semantics (
) of
Bean
we have the triple

\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket=(f,\tilde{f},b):\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket.

Then, using the backward map
b
, we can define the tuple of vectors
of values

(\bar{s},\bar{l})\triangleq b((\bar{p},\bar{k}),v)

such that
\bar{s}\vDash\Phi
and
\bar{l}\vDash\Gamma
.

From the second property of backward error lenses we then have

f{\llparenthesis{(\bar{s},\bar{l})}\rrparenthesis}_{id}=f{\llparenthesis{b((\bar{p},\bar{k}),v)}\rrparenthesis}_{id}=v.

We can now show a backward error result, i.e.,

\tilde{f}{\llparenthesis{(\bar{p},\bar{k})}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}=f{\llparenthesis{(\bar{s},\bar{l})}\rrparenthesis}_{id}
:

\displaystyle{\llparenthesis{\Phi,\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}{\llparenthesis{(\bar{p},\bar{k})}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}

\displaystyle=U_{ap}{\llbracket{\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma}\rrbracket}{\llparenthesis{(\bar{p},\bar{k})}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}

(
Lemma

18
)

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}{\llparenthesis{(\bar{p},\bar{k})}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}

(
)

\displaystyle=v

(
Theorem

8
)

\displaystyle=f{\llparenthesis{(\bar{s},\bar{l})}\rrparenthesis}_{id}

From the first property of error lenses we have

d_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}\left((\bar{p},\bar{k}),b((\bar{p},\bar{k}),v)\right)\leq d_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\left(\tilde{f}(\bar{p},\bar{k}),v\right)

so long as

\displaystyle d_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\left(\tilde{f}(\bar{p},\bar{k}),v\right)=d_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\left(v,v\right)\neq\infty.

(28)

If the base numeric type is interpreted as a metric
space with a standard distance function, then

d_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\left(v,v\right)\neq\infty
for any type

\sigma
, and so
Equation

28
is satisfied.

Unfolding definitions, and using the fact that

\tilde{f}{\llparenthesis{(\bar{p},\bar{k})}\rrparenthesis}_{ap}=v

from above, we have

\displaystyle\max\left(d_{\llbracket{\Phi}\rrbracket}(\bar{p},\bar{s}),d_{\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket}({\bar{k},\bar{l}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket}\left(v,v\right)

(29)

From
Equation

29
we can conclude two things. First,
using the definition of the distance function on discrete metric
spaces, we can conclude

\bar{p}=\bar{s}
: the discrete variables carry no backward error.
Second, for linear variables we can derive the required
backward error bound:

\displaystyle\max\left(d_{\llbracket{\sigma_{1}}\rrbracket}(k_{1},l_{1})-r_{1},\dots,d_{\llbracket{\sigma_{n}}\rrbracket}(k_{n},l_{n})-r_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 0.

∎

Appendix 6.G
Type Checking Algorithm

(Var)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma;x\Rightarrow\{x:_{0}\sigma\};\sigma

(DVar)

\Phi,z:\alpha\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};z\Rightarrow\emptyset;\alpha

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\tau

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset

(
\otimes
I)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};(e,f)\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\sigma\otimes\tau

(Unit)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};()\Rightarrow\emptyset;\mathbf{unit}

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset

(
\otimes
E
σ
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r+\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\};\sigma

where
x,y\not\in\Gamma^{\bullet}
and
r=\max\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
if at least one of

x:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},y:_{r_{2}}\tau_{2}\in\Gamma_{2}
(else
r=0
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}

\Phi,z_{1}:\alpha_{1},z_{2}:\alpha_{2}\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset

(
\otimes
E
α
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{dlet}\ {(z_{1},z_{2})}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\sigma

where
z_{1},z_{2}\not\in\Phi

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma+\tau

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma;e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\rho

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},y:\tau;f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{3};\rho

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}

=\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{3}

=\emptyset

(
+
E)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{case}\ e^{\prime}\ \mathbf{of}\ (x.e\ |\ y.f)\Rightarrow(q+\Gamma_{1}),\max\{\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x\},\Gamma_{3}\setminus\{y\}\};\rho

where
x,y\not\in\Gamma^{\bullet}
and
q=\max\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
if at least one of
x:_{q_{1}}\sigma\in\Gamma_{2}

or
y:_{q_{2}}\tau\in\Gamma_{3}
(else
q=0
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma

(
+

\text{I}_{L}
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\textbf{inl }_{\tau}\ e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma+\tau

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\tau

(
+

\text{I}_{R}
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\textbf{inr }_{\sigma}\ e\Rightarrow\sigma+\tau

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\tau

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\tau;f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset

(Let)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{let}\ {x}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r+\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x\};\sigma

where
x\not\in\Gamma^{\bullet}
and
x:_{r}\sigma\in\Gamma_{2}
(else
r=0
)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\text{num}

(Disc)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};!e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\text{dnum}

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\text{dnum}

\Phi,z:\text{dnum}\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset

(DLet)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{dlet}\ {z}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\sigma

where
z\not\in\Phi

(Add, Sub)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\text{num},y:\text{num};\{\mathbf{add},\mathbf{sub}\}\ x\ y\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\};\text{num}

(Mul)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\text{num},y:\text{num};\mathbf{mul}\ {x}\ {y}\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon/2}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon/2}\text{num}\};\text{num}

(Div)

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\text{num},y:\text{num};\{\mathbf{add},\mathbf{sub}\}\ x\ y\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\};\text{num}+\textbf{err}

(DMul)

\Phi,z:\text{dnum}\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\text{num};\mathbf{dmul}\ {z}\ {x}\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\};\text{num}

Figure 2
:
Type checking algorithm for
Bean
.

This appendix, authored by Laura Zielinski, presents the type-checking algorithm for
Bean

as described in
Section

1
, along with proofs of its soundness and completeness.
First, we give the full type checking algorithm in
Figure

2
.
Recall that algorithm calls are written as
\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma

where
\Gamma^{\bullet}
is a linear context skeleton,
e
is a
Bean
program,

\Gamma
is, intuitively, the
minimal
linear context required to type
e
such that

\overline{\Gamma}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
, and
\sigma
is the type of
e
.
Note that we only require
\Phi
to contain the discrete variables used in the program
and we do nothing more; thus, it is not returned by the algorithm.
We do require that discrete and linear contexts are always disjoint, and we will denote
linear variables by
x
and
y
and discrete variables by
z
.
Finally, we define the
max
of two linear contexts,
\max\{\Gamma,\Delta\}
,
to have domain
\text{dom}\Gamma\cup\text{dom}\Delta
and, if
x:_{q}\sigma\in\Gamma
and
x:_{r}\sigma\in\Delta
,
then
x:_{\max\{q,r\}}\sigma\in\max\{\Gamma,\Delta\}
.

Before we give proofs of
Theorem

11
and
Theorem

12
,
we must prove two lemmas about type system and algorithm weakening.
Intuitively, type system weakening says that if we can derive the type of a program from a context
\Gamma
,
then we can also derive the same program from a larger context
\Delta
which subsumes
\Gamma
.

Lemma 26

(Type System Weakening)
.

If
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
and
\Gamma\sqsubseteq\Delta
, then
\Phi\mid\Delta\vdash e:\sigma
.

Proof.

Suppose
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
. We proceed by induction on the final typing rule applied
and show some representative cases below.

Case (Var).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma\vdash x:\sigma.

Let
\Delta
be a context such that
(\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma)\sqsubseteq\Delta
. Thus,
x:_{q}\sigma\in\Delta

where
r\leq q
. By the same rule,
\Phi\mid\Delta\vdash x:\sigma
.

Case (
\otimes
I).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau

and thus, we also have that

\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Delta\vdash f:\tau.

Let
\Lambda
be a context such that
(\Gamma,\Delta)\sqsubseteq\Lambda
.
As
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint, we can split
\Lambda
into the contexts

\Gamma_{1}
and
\Delta_{1}
such that
\Gamma\sqsubseteq\Gamma_{1}
and
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Delta_{1}
.
By out inductive hypothesis, it follows that

\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash e:\sigma\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Delta_{1}\vdash f:\tau.

By the same rule, we conclude that

\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1},\Delta_{1}\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau.

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

Suppose the last rule applied
was

\Phi\mid r+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma.

Let
\Lambda
be a context such that
(r+\Gamma,\Delta)\sqsubseteq\Lambda
and

x,y\not\in\text{dom}\Lambda
. As before, split
\Lambda
into contexts
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Delta_{1}

such that
(r+\Gamma)\sqsubseteq\Gamma_{1}
and
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Delta_{1}
but where

\text{dom}\Gamma=\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}
. Now, for each
x\in\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}
, we have that
x:_{q}\sigma\in\Gamma_{1}

where
r\leq q
. Therefore, we can define the context
-r+\Gamma_{1}
which subtracts
r
from the
error bound of every variable in
\Gamma_{1}
, and hence
\Gamma\sqsubseteq(-r+\Gamma_{1})
.
Finally, use our inductive hypothesis to get that

\Phi\mid(-r+\Gamma_{1})\vdash e:\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Delta_{1},x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2}\vdash f:\sigma

and we can apply the same rule to get our conclusion.

Case (Add).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon+r_{1}}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon+r_{2}}\text{num}\vdash\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}:\text{num}

Let
\Delta
be a context such that

(\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon+r_{1}}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon+r_{2}}\text{num})\sqsubseteq\Delta
.
Hence,
x:_{q_{1}}\text{num},y:_{q_{2}}\text{num}\in\Delta
where
\varepsilon+r_{1}\leq q_{1}
and
\varepsilon+r_{2}\leq q_{2}
.
Rewrite
q_{1}=\varepsilon+(q_{1}-\varepsilon)
and
q_{2}=\varepsilon+(q_{2}-\varepsilon)
and apply the same rule.

∎

Similarly, algorithm weakening says that if we pass a context skeleton
\Gamma^{\bullet}
into the algorithm and it
infers context
\Gamma
, then if we pass in a larger skeleton
\Delta^{\bullet}
, the algorithm will
still infer context
\Gamma
. (Here, we extend the notion of subcontexts to context skeletons, where

\Gamma^{\bullet}\sqsubseteq\Delta^{\bullet}
if
\Gamma^{\bullet}\subseteq\Delta^{\bullet}
.)
This is because the algorithm discards unused variables from the context.

Lemma 27

(Type Checking Algorithm Weakening)
.

If
\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma
and
\Gamma^{\bullet}\sqsubseteq\Delta^{\bullet}
,
then
\Phi\mid\Delta^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma
.
We proceed by induction on the final algorithmic step applied and
show some representative cases below.

Case (Var).

Suppose the last step applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma;x\Rightarrow\{x:_{0}\sigma\};\sigma.

Let
\Delta^{\bullet}
be a context skeleton such that

(\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma)\sqsubseteq\Delta^{\bullet}
. Thus,
x:\sigma\in\Delta^{\bullet}

so we can apply the same rule.

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

Suppose the last step applied
was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r+\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\};\sigma.

Let
\Delta^{\bullet}
be a context skeleton such that
\Gamma^{\bullet}\sqsubseteq\Delta^{\bullet}

and
x,y\not\in\Delta^{\bullet}
. By induction, we have that

\Phi\mid\Delta^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Delta^{\bullet},x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

and
\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
. By the same rule, we conclude that

\Phi\mid\Delta^{\bullet};\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r+\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\};\sigma.

∎

Finally, we give proofs of algorithmic soundness and completeness.
Soundness states that if the algorithm returns a linear context
\Gamma
, then we can use
\Gamma

to derive the program using
Bean
’s type system.

Theorem

11
.

If
\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma
, then

\overline{\Gamma}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
and the derivation

\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
exists.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma;\sigma
.
We proceed by induction on the final algorithmic step applied and show some
representative cases below. We use the fact that if
\Gamma,\Delta
are disjoint,
then
\overline{\Gamma,\Delta}=\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\Delta}
.

Case (Var).

Suppose the last step applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma;x\Rightarrow\{x:_{0}\sigma\};\sigma.

By the typing rule (Var
σ
), we have that
\Phi\mid\{x:_{0}\sigma\}\vdash x:\sigma
.
Moreover,

\overline{\{x:_{0}\sigma\}}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma)
.

Case (
\otimes
I).

Suppose the last step applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};(e,f)\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\sigma\otimes\tau

where

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};f\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2};\sigma

and
\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
. By our inductive hypothesis,
we have that
\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash e:\sigma
and
\Phi\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash f:\tau
.
Therefore, we can apply the typing rule (
\otimes
I) to get that

\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau.

Finally, as
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
and

\overline{\Gamma_{2}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
,
we have that
\overline{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
.

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

Suppose the last step applied
was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r+\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\};\sigma

By induction, we have that

\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash e:\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash f:\sigma,

where
x,y
may be in
\text{dom}\Gamma_{2}
.
Let
\Delta=\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\}
.
Since
r
is defined to be the maximum of the bounds on
x,y
if they exist in

\Gamma_{2}
, we have that
\Gamma_{2}\sqsubseteq(\Delta,x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2})
.
From
Lemma

26
, it follows that

\Phi\mid\Delta,x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2}\vdash f:\sigma.

Thus, we can apply the typing rule (
\otimes
E
σ
) to conclude that

\Phi\mid r+\Gamma_{1},\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma.

Finally, as
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
and

\overline{\Gamma_{2}}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2})
, we have that

\overline{r+\Gamma_{1},\Delta}=\overline{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\}}=\overline{\Gamma_{1}},\overline{\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x,y\}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}.

Case (+ E).

Suppose the last step applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet};\mathbf{case}\ e^{\prime}\ \mathbf{of}\ (x.e\ |\ y.f)\Rightarrow(q+\Gamma_{1}),\max\{\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x\},\Gamma_{3}\setminus\{y\}\};\rho.

By induction, we have that

\Phi\mid\Gamma_{1}\vdash e^{\prime}:\sigma+\tau\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Gamma_{2}\vdash e:\rho\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Gamma_{3}\vdash f:\rho.

Let
\Delta=\max\{\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x\},\Gamma_{3}\setminus\{y\}\}
, and we
still have that
\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Delta=\emptyset
. By
Lemma

26
,
it follows that

\Phi\mid\Delta,x:_{q}\sigma\vdash e:\rho\text{ and }\Phi\mid\Delta,y:_{q}\tau\vdash f:\rho

by weakening the bounds on
x
and
y
to
q
. Thus, we can apply typing rule (+ E) to
conclude that

\Phi\mid q+\Gamma_{1},\Delta\vdash\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}:\rho.

Moreover, as
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}
and

\overline{\Gamma_{2}}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\sigma)
and

\overline{\Gamma_{3}}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma^{\bullet},y:\tau)
, we have that

\overline{q+\Gamma_{1},\Delta}=\overline{\Gamma_{1}},\overline{\max\{\Gamma_{2}\setminus\{x\},\Gamma_{3}\setminus\{y\}\}}\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\bullet}.

Case (Add).

Suppose the last step applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma^{\bullet},x:\text{num},y:\text{num};\mathbf{add}\ x\ y\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\};\text{num}.

By the typing rule (Add) we have that

\Phi\mid\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\}\vdash\mathbf{add}~x~y:\text{num}.

∎

Conversely, completeness says that if from
\Gamma
we can derive the type of a program
e
, then
inputting
\overline{\Gamma}
and
e
into the algorithm will yield a valid output.

Theorem

12
.

If
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
is a valid derivation in
Bean
, then there
exists a context
\Delta\sqsubseteq\Gamma
such that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma};e\Rightarrow\Delta;\sigma
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Phi\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\sigma
. We proceed by induction on the final typing rule applied
and show some representative cases below.

Case (Var
σ
).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma\vdash x:\sigma.

By algorithm step (Var), we have that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma},x:\sigma;x\Rightarrow\{x:_{0}\sigma\};\sigma

and
\{x:_{0}\sigma\}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma,x:_{r}\sigma)
as
0\leq r
.

Case (
\otimes
I).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,\Delta\vdash(e,f):\sigma\otimes\tau.

From this, we deduce that
\text{dom}\Gamma\cap\text{dom}\Delta=\emptyset
. By induction, there exist

\Gamma_{1}\sqsubseteq\Gamma
and
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq\Delta
such that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma\text{ and }\Phi\mid\overline{\Delta};f\Rightarrow\Delta_{1};\tau.

Moreover,
\text{dom}\Gamma_{1}\cap\text{dom}\Delta_{1}=\emptyset
as well.
By
Lemma

27
, we also have that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma,\Delta};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma\text{ and }\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma,\Delta};f\Rightarrow\Delta_{1};\tau.

Thus, we can apply algorithm step
(
\otimes
I) to conclude

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma,\Delta};(e,f)\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1},\Delta_{1};\sigma\otimes\tau

where we know
(\Gamma_{1},\Delta_{1})\sqsubseteq(\Gamma,\Delta)
.

Case (
\otimes
E
σ
).

Suppose the last rule applied
was

\Phi\mid r+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}:\sigma.

By induction, there exist
\Gamma_{1}\sqsubseteq\Gamma
and

\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq(\Delta,x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2})
such that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma};e\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\tau_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}\text{ and }\Phi\mid\overline{\Delta},x:\tau_{1},y:\tau_{2};f\Rightarrow\Delta_{1};\sigma.

If
x:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},y:_{r_{2}}\tau_{2}\in\Delta_{1}
, let
r^{\prime}=\max\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
. As

\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq(\Delta,x:_{r}\tau_{1},y:_{r}\tau_{2})
, we know
r^{\prime}\leq r
.

Using
Lemma

27
, we can apply algorithm step (
\otimes
E
σ
) of

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma,\Delta};\mathbf{let}\ {(x,y)}={e}\ \mathbf{in}\ {f}\Rightarrow(r^{\prime}+\Gamma_{1}),\Delta_{1}\setminus\{x,y\};\sigma.

Moreover,
(r^{\prime}+\Gamma_{1})\sqsubseteq(r+\Gamma)
and
(\Delta_{1}\setminus\{x,y\})\sqsubseteq\Delta
.

Case (+ E).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid q+\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}:\rho.

By induction, there exist
\Gamma_{1}\sqsubseteq\Gamma
,
\Delta_{1}\sqsubseteq(\Delta,x:_{q}\sigma)
, and

\Delta_{2}\sqsubseteq(\Delta,y:_{q}\tau)
such that

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma};e^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1};\sigma+\tau\text{ and }\Phi\mid\overline{\Delta},x:\sigma;e\Rightarrow\Delta_{1};\rho\text{ and }\Phi\mid\overline{\Delta},y:\tau;f\Rightarrow\Delta_{2};\rho.

If
x:_{q_{1}}\sigma\in\Delta_{1}
and
y:_{q_{2}}\tau\in\Delta_{2}
, let
q^{\prime}=\max\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
,
and we know
q^{\prime}\leq q
. Using
Lemma

27
, we can apply algorithm step (+ E) of

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma,\Delta};\linec{{{case}}~{$e^{\prime}$}~{of}~({inl }{$x.e$} {}$\mid${} {inr }{$y.f$})}\Rightarrow(q^{\prime}+\Gamma_{1}),\max\{\Delta_{1}\setminus\{x\},\Delta_{2}\setminus\{y\}\};\rho.

Furthermore, we know
(q^{\prime}+\Gamma_{1})\sqsubseteq(q+\Gamma)
and

\max\{\Delta_{1}\setminus\{x\},\Delta_{2}\setminus\{y\}\}\sqsubseteq\Delta
.

Case (Add).

Suppose the last rule applied was

\Phi\mid\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon+r_{1}}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon+r_{2}}\text{num}\vdash\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}:\text{num}

where
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
. We can apply algorithm step (Add) of

\Phi\mid\overline{\Gamma},x:\text{num},y:\text{num};\mathbf{add}\ {x}\ {y}\Rightarrow\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\};\text{num}

and we have
\{x:_{\varepsilon}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon}\text{num}\}\sqsubseteq(\Gamma,x:_{\varepsilon+r_{1}}\text{num},y:_{\varepsilon+r_{2}}\text{num})
.

∎
